Yeah, um… honestly, I’m here because of my work physical. I’m a mechanic, and they had me do the usual checkup for the job, and the doctor there kind of made a bigger deal out of my weight than I expected. He said it was too low and that I should come get it looked into more.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.

It’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. Same with basketball — I don’t want to feel heavy running up and down the court. So yeah… I don’t like the idea of gaining weight. It makes me feel like I’d be less able to move the way I want to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess it does, if I’m being honest. I don’t always think of it like “I’m changing my eating because I’m worried about weight,” but that’s probably what’s happening.

Most mornings I just do coffee. That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. There’s always something running late or somebody needs help with a job, or I’m under a car and don’t want to stop and eat. Later I might have a small sandwich, or like half of whatever’s around, but I try to keep it pretty simple.

Part of it is work being busy, and part of it is the house being busy too, so I tell myself it’s just easier this way. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.

Most days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.

And at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.

Like, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.

And then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.

Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. Same with basketball — I don’t want to feel heavy running up and down the court. So yeah… I don’t like the idea of gaining weight. It makes me feel like I’d be less able to move the way I want to.\n\nDoctor: Does the thought of gaining weight affect how you eat or what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, if I’m being honest. I don’t always think of it like “I’m changing my eating because I’m worried about weight,” but that’s probably what’s happening.\n\nMost mornings I just do coffee. That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. There’s always something running late or somebody needs help with a job, or I’m under a car and don’t want to stop and eat. Later I might have a small sandwich, or like half of whatever’s around, but I try to keep it pretty simple.\n\nPart of it is work being busy, and part of it is the house being busy too, so I tell myself it’s just easier this way. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.\n\nMost days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.

Like if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.

I’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.

So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.

If I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.

So yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.

So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.

If something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.

Then it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.

So yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.

So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.

It’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.

It’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.

He said it was too low and that I should come get it looked into more.\n\nI guess I don’t really feel like it’s a problem the way he seemed to. I mean, I feel lighter, if anything. I still play basketball, and being lighter on my feet actually feels kind of helpful out there. And at the garage, crawling around, bending, being under cars, moving around all day… I don’t feel as weighed down, you know? So it’s a little weird to be told it’s an issue when to me it’s felt more like I’m moving better. But they wanted me to follow up, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. Same with basketball — I don’t want to feel heavy running up and down the court. So yeah… I don’t like the idea of gaining weight. It makes me feel like I’d be less able to move the way I want to.\n\nDoctor: Does the thought of gaining weight affect how you eat or what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, if I’m being honest. I don’t always think of it like “I’m changing my eating because I’m worried about weight,” but that’s probably what’s happening.\n\nMost mornings I just do coffee. That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. There’s always something running late or somebody needs help with a job, or I’m under a car and don’t want to stop and eat. Later I might have a small sandwich, or like half of whatever’s around, but I try to keep it pretty simple.\n\nPart of it is work being busy, and part of it is the house being busy too, so I tell myself it’s just easier this way. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.\n\nMost days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"If I see the scale bump up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft and that I’ll be slower moving around cars or up and down the court.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.

Like, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.

And then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… honestly, I’m here because of my work physical. I’m a mechanic, and they had me do the usual checkup for the job, and the doctor there kind of made a bigger deal out of my weight than I expected. He said it was too low and that I should come get it looked into more.\n\nI guess I don’t really feel like it’s a problem the way he seemed to. I mean, I feel lighter, if anything. I still play basketball, and being lighter on my feet actually feels kind of helpful out there. And at the garage, crawling around, bending, being under cars, moving around all day… I don’t feel as weighed down, you know? So it’s a little weird to be told it’s an issue when to me it’s felt more like I’m moving better. But they wanted me to follow up, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. Same with basketball — I don’t want to feel heavy running up and down the court. So yeah… I don’t like the idea of gaining weight. It makes me feel like I’d be less able to move the way I want to.\n\nDoctor: Does the thought of gaining weight affect how you eat or what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, if I’m being honest. I don’t always think of it like “I’m changing my eating because I’m worried about weight,” but that’s probably what’s happening.\n\nMost mornings I just do coffee. That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. There’s always something running late or somebody needs help with a job, or I’m under a car and don’t want to stop and eat. Later I might have a small sandwich, or like half of whatever’s around, but I try to keep it pretty simple.\n\nPart of it is work being busy, and part of it is the house being busy too, so I tell myself it’s just easier this way. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.\n\nMost days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess there is a difference. I mean, people around me act like I’m thinner than I think I am, or like it’s more serious than I feel like it is.

I know they’re worried — I’m not saying they’re making it up or anything. But part of me just thinks they’re making too much out of it, because I’m still doing my normal stuff. I still get up and go to work, I’m still getting through the day, working on cars, doing what needs to get done. I take care of things at home. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.

Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. So when someone says I look too thin or they’re worried about my weight, I hear it, but I don’t fully… I don’t know, I don’t fully buy it the same way they do. To me it feels like I’m managing, and maybe they’re just seeing it differently or overreacting a bit.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… honestly, I’m here because of my work physical. I’m a mechanic, and they had me do the usual checkup for the job, and the doctor there kind of made a bigger deal out of my weight than I expected. He said it was too low and that I should come get it looked into more.\n\nI guess I don’t really feel like it’s a problem the way he seemed to. I mean, I feel lighter, if anything. I still play basketball, and being lighter on my feet actually feels kind of helpful out there. And at the garage, crawling around, bending, being under cars, moving around all day… I don’t feel as weighed down, you know? So it’s a little weird to be told it’s an issue when to me it’s felt more like I’m moving better. But they wanted me to follow up, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. Same with basketball — I don’t want to feel heavy running up and down the court. So yeah… I don’t like the idea of gaining weight. It makes me feel like I’d be less able to move the way I want to.\n\nDoctor: Does the thought of gaining weight affect how you eat or what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, if I’m being honest. I don’t always think of it like “I’m changing my eating because I’m worried about weight,” but that’s probably what’s happening.\n\nMost mornings I just do coffee. That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. There’s always something running late or somebody needs help with a job, or I’m under a car and don’t want to stop and eat. Later I might have a small sandwich, or like half of whatever’s around, but I try to keep it pretty simple.\n\nPart of it is work being busy, and part of it is the house being busy too, so I tell myself it’s just easier this way. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.\n\nMost days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is a difference. I mean, people around me act like I’m thinner than I think I am, or like it’s more serious than I feel like it is.\n\nI know they’re worried — I’m not saying they’re making it up or anything. I still get up and go to work, I’m still getting through the day, working on cars, doing what needs to get done. I take care of things at home. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.\n\nLike, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. So when someone says I look too thin or they’re worried about my weight, I hear it, but I don’t fully… I don’t know, I don’t fully buy it the same way they do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s kind of both, honestly, and they feed into each other for me.

Like, the first thing that usually sets me off is something small — if I see the scale go up even one pound, or if my jeans feel a little tighter around the waist. Then my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” So there’s definitely a look part to it, like how I think my body is changing.

But almost right after that, I start thinking about how it’ll affect what I can do. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.

So I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.

I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is a difference. I mean, people around me act like I’m thinner than I think I am, or like it’s more serious than I feel like it is.\n\nI know they’re worried — I’m not saying they’re making it up or anything. But part of me just thinks they’re making too much out of it, because I’m still doing my normal stuff. I still get up and go to work, I’m still getting through the day, working on cars, doing what needs to get done. I take care of things at home. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.\n\nLike, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. So when someone says I look too thin or they’re worried about my weight, I hear it, but I don’t fully… I don’t know, I don’t fully buy it the same way they do. To me it feels like I’m managing, and maybe they’re just seeing it differently or overreacting a bit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or more about how you think you would perform physically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly, and they feed into each other for me.\n\nLike, the first thing that usually sets me off is something small — if I see the scale go up even one pound, or if my jeans feel a little tighter around the waist. Then my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” So there’s definitely a look part to it, like how I think my body is changing.\n\nBut almost right after that, I start thinking about how it’ll affect what I can do. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"If I see the scale bump up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft and that I’ll be slower moving around cars or up and down the court.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. It starts with how I think I look, like if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter than they did before, especially around the waist. Then I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and that bothers me.

But then almost right away, I turn it into a performance thing too. Like at work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, reaching, getting into awkward spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying that if I gain weight I’m going to feel slower or less comfortable doing all that. And on the court, same thing — I picture myself running up and down and feeling heavier, not as quick.

So staying where I am feels like… I don’t know, like I’m keeping myself sharp, I guess. Looking a certain way is part of it, but I think being able to move the way I want is a big part too.
