========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… honestly, I’m here because of my work physical. I’m a mechanic, and they had me do the usual checkup for the job, and the doctor there kind of made a bigger deal out of my weight than I expected. He said it was too low and that I should come get it looked into more.\n\nI guess I don’t really feel like it’s a problem the way he seemed to. I mean, I feel lighter, if anything. I still play basketball, and being lighter on my feet actually feels kind of helpful out there. And at the garage, crawling around, bending, being under cars, moving around all day… I don’t feel as weighed down, you know? So it’s a little weird to be told it’s an issue when to me it’s felt more like I’m moving better. But they wanted me to follow up, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. Same with basketball — I don’t want to feel heavy running up and down the court. So yeah… I don’t like the idea of gaining weight. It makes me feel like I’d be less able to move the way I want to.\n\nDoctor: Does the thought of gaining weight affect how you eat or what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, if I’m being honest. I don’t always think of it like “I’m changing my eating because I’m worried about weight,” but that’s probably what’s happening.\n\nMost mornings I just do coffee. That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. There’s always something running late or somebody needs help with a job, or I’m under a car and don’t want to stop and eat. Later I might have a small sandwich, or like half of whatever’s around, but I try to keep it pretty simple.\n\nPart of it is work being busy, and part of it is the house being busy too, so I tell myself it’s just easier this way. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.\n\nMost days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is a difference. I mean, people around me act like I’m thinner than I think I am, or like it’s more serious than I feel like it is.\n\nI know they’re worried — I’m not saying they’re making it up or anything. But part of me just thinks they’re making too much out of it, because I’m still doing my normal stuff. I still get up and go to work, I’m still getting through the day, working on cars, doing what needs to get done. I take care of things at home. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.\n\nLike, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. So when someone says I look too thin or they’re worried about my weight, I hear it, but I don’t fully… I don’t know, I don’t fully buy it the same way they do. To me it feels like I’m managing, and maybe they’re just seeing it differently or overreacting a bit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or more about how you think you would perform physically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly, and they feed into each other for me.\n\nLike, the first thing that usually sets me off is something small — if I see the scale go up even one pound, or if my jeans feel a little tighter around the waist. Then my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” So there’s definitely a look part to it, like how I think my body is changing.\n\nBut almost right after that, I start thinking about how it’ll affect what I can do. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. It starts with how I think I look, like if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter than they did before, especially around the waist. Then I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and that bothers me.\n\nBut then almost right away, I turn it into a performance thing too. Like at work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, reaching, getting into awkward spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying that if I gain weight I’m going to feel slower or less comfortable doing all that. And on the court, same thing — I picture myself running up and down and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo staying where I am feels like… I don’t know, like I’m keeping myself sharp, I guess. Looking a certain way is part of it, but I think being able to move the way I want is a big part too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I mean… I guess if you go by what the doctor at my work physical said, then yeah. That’s actually how I ended up here in the first place. I went in thinking it was just going to be the usual kind of checkup for work, and then he made a point of saying my weight was too low for my height and build.

So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. To me it feels like I’m managing, and maybe they’re just seeing it differently or overreacting a bit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or more about how you think you would perform physically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly, and they feed into each other for me.\n\nLike, the first thing that usually sets me off is something small — if I see the scale go up even one pound, or if my jeans feel a little tighter around the waist. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. It starts with how I think I look, like if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter than they did before, especially around the waist. Then I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and that bothers me.\n\nBut then almost right away, I turn it into a performance thing too. Like at work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, reaching, getting into awkward spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying that if I gain weight I’m going to feel slower or less comfortable doing all that. And on the court, same thing — I picture myself running up and down and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo staying where I am feels like… I don’t know, like I’m keeping myself sharp, I guess. Looking a certain way is part of it, but I think being able to move the way I want is a big part too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess if you go by what the doctor at my work physical said, then yeah. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when I look in the mirror, I feel like I’m seeing what’s there… but then I also know I can get really focused on certain parts.

Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think I’m seeing a completely different person or anything like that. It’s more that I notice things maybe other people wouldn’t notice as much — like around my waist, or whether I look softer than I did before, or if my clothes sit differently. And then I start questioning it, like, “Is that actually how I look, or am I just zeroing in on it too much?”

So… significantly different? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m hallucinating some different body in the mirror. But I also don’t fully trust myself to be objective about it, if that makes sense.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… honestly, I’m here because of my work physical. I’m a mechanic, and they had me do the usual checkup for the job, and the doctor there kind of made a bigger deal out of my weight than I expected. He said it was too low and that I should come get it looked into more.\n\nI guess I don’t really feel like it’s a problem the way he seemed to. I mean, I feel lighter, if anything. I still play basketball, and being lighter on my feet actually feels kind of helpful out there. And at the garage, crawling around, bending, being under cars, moving around all day… I don’t feel as weighed down, you know? So it’s a little weird to be told it’s an issue when to me it’s felt more like I’m moving better. But they wanted me to follow up, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. Same with basketball — I don’t want to feel heavy running up and down the court. So yeah… I don’t like the idea of gaining weight. It makes me feel like I’d be less able to move the way I want to.\n\nDoctor: Does the thought of gaining weight affect how you eat or what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does, if I’m being honest. I don’t always think of it like “I’m changing my eating because I’m worried about weight,” but that’s probably what’s happening.\n\nMost mornings I just do coffee. That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. There’s always something running late or somebody needs help with a job, or I’m under a car and don’t want to stop and eat. Later I might have a small sandwich, or like half of whatever’s around, but I try to keep it pretty simple.\n\nPart of it is work being busy, and part of it is the house being busy too, so I tell myself it’s just easier this way. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.\n\nMost days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is a difference. I mean, people around me act like I’m thinner than I think I am, or like it’s more serious than I feel like it is.\n\nI know they’re worried — I’m not saying they’re making it up or anything. But part of me just thinks they’re making too much out of it, because I’m still doing my normal stuff. I still get up and go to work, I’m still getting through the day, working on cars, doing what needs to get done. I take care of things at home. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.\n\nLike, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. So when someone says I look too thin or they’re worried about my weight, I hear it, but I don’t fully… I don’t know, I don’t fully buy it the same way they do. To me it feels like I’m managing, and maybe they’re just seeing it differently or overreacting a bit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or more about how you think you would perform physically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly, and they feed into each other for me.\n\nLike, the first thing that usually sets me off is something small — if I see the scale go up even one pound, or if my jeans feel a little tighter around the waist. Then my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” So there’s definitely a look part to it, like how I think my body is changing.\n\nBut almost right after that, I start thinking about how it’ll affect what I can do. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. It starts with how I think I look, like if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter than they did before, especially around the waist. Then I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and that bothers me.\n\nBut then almost right away, I turn it into a performance thing too. Like at work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, reaching, getting into awkward spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying that if I gain weight I’m going to feel slower or less comfortable doing all that. And on the court, same thing — I picture myself running up and down and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo staying where I am feels like… I don’t know, like I’m keeping myself sharp, I guess. Looking a certain way is part of it, but I think being able to move the way I want is a big part too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess if you go by what the doctor at my work physical said, then yeah. That’s actually how I ended up here in the first place. I went in thinking it was just going to be the usual kind of checkup for work, and then he made a point of saying my weight was too low for my height and build.\n\nBut in my head, it doesn’t feel that way. Like, I hear “too low,” and I understand what he’s saying, but I don’t feel like I’m walking around sick or anything. I feel lighter, if that makes sense. On the basketball court, I feel like I can move better, like I’m quicker on my feet and not dragging extra weight around. And at the garage, being on my feet all day, getting under hoods, moving around cars, bending and reaching — I feel like I’m not as weighed down.\n\nSo… yeah, other people, or at least the work doctor, would probably say it’s lower than normal. I just don’t always see it that way myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is significantly different from what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when I look in the mirror, I feel like I’m seeing what’s there… but then I also know I can get really focused on certain parts.\n\nLike, I don’t look in the mirror and think I’m seeing a completely different person or anything like that. It’s more that I notice things maybe other people wouldn’t notice as much — like around my waist, or whether I look softer than I did before, or if my clothes sit differently. And then I start questioning it, like, “Is that actually how I look, or am I just zeroing in on it too much?”\n\nSo… significantly different? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m hallucinating some different body in the mirror. But I also don’t fully trust myself to be objective about it, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is a difference. I mean, people around me act like I’m thinner than I think I am, or like it’s more serious than I feel like it is.\n\nI know they’re worried — I’m not saying they’re making it up or anything. But part of me just thinks they’re making too much out of it, because I’m still doing my normal stuff. I still get up and go to work, I’m still getting through the day, working on cars, doing what needs to get done. I take care of things at home. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.\n\nLike, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. So when someone says I look too thin or they’re worried about my weight, I hear it, but I don’t fully… I don’t know, I don’t fully buy it the same way they do. To me it feels like I’m managing, and maybe they’re just seeing it differently or overreacting a bit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or more about how you think you would perform physically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly, and they feed into each other for me.\n\nLike, the first thing that usually sets me off is something small — if I see the scale go up even one pound, or if my jeans feel a little tighter around the waist. Then my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” So there’s definitely a look part to it, like how I think my body is changing.\n\nBut almost right after that, I start thinking about how it’ll affect what I can do. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. It starts with how I think I look, like if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter than they did before, especially around the waist. Then I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and that bothers me.\n\nBut then almost right away, I turn it into a performance thing too. Like at work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, reaching, getting into awkward spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying that if I gain weight I’m going to feel slower or less comfortable doing all that. And on the court, same thing — I picture myself running up and down and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo staying where I am feels like… I don’t know, like I’m keeping myself sharp, I guess. Looking a certain way is part of it, but I think being able to move the way I want is a big part too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess if you go by what the doctor at my work physical said, then yeah. That’s actually how I ended up here in the first place. I went in thinking it was just going to be the usual kind of checkup for work, and then he made a point of saying my weight was too low for my height and build.\n\nBut in my head, it doesn’t feel that way. Like, I hear “too low,” and I understand what he’s saying, but I don’t feel like I’m walking around sick or anything. I feel lighter, if that makes sense. On the basketball court, I feel like I can move better, like I’m quicker on my feet and not dragging extra weight around. And at the garage, being on my feet all day, getting under hoods, moving around cars, bending and reaching — I feel like I’m not as weighed down.\n\nSo… yeah, other people, or at least the work doctor, would probably say it’s lower than normal. I just don’t always see it that way myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is significantly different from what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when I look in the mirror, I feel like I’m seeing what’s there… but then I also know I can get really focused on certain parts.\n\nLike, I don’t look in the mirror and think I’m seeing a completely different person or anything like that. It’s more that I notice things maybe other people wouldn’t notice as much — like around my waist, or whether I look softer than I did before, or if my clothes sit differently. And then I start questioning it, like, “Is that actually how I look, or am I just zeroing in on it too much?”\n\nSo… significantly different? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m hallucinating some different body in the mirror. But I also don’t fully trust myself to be objective about it, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s kind of tied together for me, honestly. It starts more with appearance, I think — like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, or my jeans feel tighter than usual, especially around my waist, my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” I know that probably sounds like a small thing, but it can really get in my head.

And then almost immediately I connect that to performance too. Like, I start thinking if I’m softer or heavier, then I’m going to be slower moving around at work — getting in and out around cars, bending, reaching, being on my feet — like I won’t move as easily. Same thing with basketball. I picture myself going up and down the court and feeling heavier or not as quick.

So I guess the fear is both. The appearance part hits first, but then I justify it to myself as, “No, this is about staying quick and able to do what I need to do.” It all kind of feeds into the same worry.

That’s kind of my routine now — get up, get out the door, have coffee, and once I’m at the shop it’s easy to just keep going. But I do have these little rules in my head, I guess. Like if I keep it light and basic, I feel better about it. I feel more in control, and I don’t have to sit there wondering if I overdid it. So yeah, the thought of gaining weight does affect what I eat, even if I don’t always call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about how it might affect your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t know if it’s just appearance exactly, like looking in the mirror or whatever, but if I wasn’t thinking about getting heavier or feeling bigger, I’d probably eat more than I do now.\n\nMost days I kind of keep it to coffee in the morning. Then once I’m at the shop, I get busy, and it’s easy to just say, “I’ll eat later,” because there’s always a car that’s taking longer than it should or somebody needs a hand. Later on I might have a small sandwich, or honestly sometimes just half of whatever is around, and I tell myself that’s enough.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I sort of use that as another reason to keep it simple. But if I’m being straight with you, I do have these rules in my head about keeping things light. It feels better to stick to them, like I’m not overdoing it. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. It kind of all runs together in my head.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a pound, I notice it, but it’s not just the number. I start wondering where it’s going to show up. If my jeans feel tighter around the waist, that really gets to me. Then I’m thinking, okay, I’m getting soft, I’m losing that lean feeling, and I don’t like that.\n\nAnd then I connect that to how I move, too. Like at work, I’m crawling under cars, bending, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into awkward spots. If I feel bigger around the middle, I start thinking I’m going to be slower or clumsier. Same thing playing basketball — if I feel heavy or soft, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is a difference. I mean, people around me act like I’m thinner than I think I am, or like it’s more serious than I feel like it is.\n\nI know they’re worried — I’m not saying they’re making it up or anything. But part of me just thinks they’re making too much out of it, because I’m still doing my normal stuff. I still get up and go to work, I’m still getting through the day, working on cars, doing what needs to get done. I take care of things at home. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.\n\nLike, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. So when someone says I look too thin or they’re worried about my weight, I hear it, but I don’t fully… I don’t know, I don’t fully buy it the same way they do. To me it feels like I’m managing, and maybe they’re just seeing it differently or overreacting a bit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or more about how you think you would perform physically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly, and they feed into each other for me.\n\nLike, the first thing that usually sets me off is something small — if I see the scale go up even one pound, or if my jeans feel a little tighter around the waist. Then my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” So there’s definitely a look part to it, like how I think my body is changing.\n\nBut almost right after that, I start thinking about how it’ll affect what I can do. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. It starts with how I think I look, like if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter than they did before, especially around the waist. Then I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and that bothers me.\n\nBut then almost right away, I turn it into a performance thing too. Like at work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, reaching, getting into awkward spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying that if I gain weight I’m going to feel slower or less comfortable doing all that. And on the court, same thing — I picture myself running up and down and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo staying where I am feels like… I don’t know, like I’m keeping myself sharp, I guess. Looking a certain way is part of it, but I think being able to move the way I want is a big part too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess if you go by what the doctor at my work physical said, then yeah. That’s actually how I ended up here in the first place. I went in thinking it was just going to be the usual kind of checkup for work, and then he made a point of saying my weight was too low for my height and build.\n\nBut in my head, it doesn’t feel that way. Like, I hear “too low,” and I understand what he’s saying, but I don’t feel like I’m walking around sick or anything. I feel lighter, if that makes sense. On the basketball court, I feel like I can move better, like I’m quicker on my feet and not dragging extra weight around. And at the garage, being on my feet all day, getting under hoods, moving around cars, bending and reaching — I feel like I’m not as weighed down.\n\nSo… yeah, other people, or at least the work doctor, would probably say it’s lower than normal. I just don’t always see it that way myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is significantly different from what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when I look in the mirror, I feel like I’m seeing what’s there… but then I also know I can get really focused on certain parts.\n\nLike, I don’t look in the mirror and think I’m seeing a completely different person or anything like that. It’s more that I notice things maybe other people wouldn’t notice as much — like around my waist, or whether I look softer than I did before, or if my clothes sit differently. And then I start questioning it, like, “Is that actually how I look, or am I just zeroing in on it too much?”\n\nSo… significantly different? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m hallucinating some different body in the mirror. But I also don’t fully trust myself to be objective about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think it would affect your appearance or how you think it would affect your ability to perform daily activities and sports?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of tied together for me, honestly. It starts more with appearance, I think — like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, or my jeans feel tighter than usual, especially around my waist, my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” I know that probably sounds like a small thing, but it can really get in my head.\n\nAnd then almost immediately I connect that to performance too. Like, I start thinking if I’m softer or heavier, then I’m going to be slower moving around at work — getting in and out around cars, bending, reaching, being on my feet — like I won’t move as easily. Same thing with basketball. I picture myself going up and down the court and feeling heavier or not as quick.\n\nSo I guess the fear is both. The appearance part hits first, but then I justify it to myself as, “No, this is about staying quick and able to do what I need to do.” It all kind of feeds into the same worry.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It starts more with appearance, I think — like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, or my jeans feel tighter than usual, especially around my waist, my brain goes straight to, “I’m getting soft.” I know that probably sounds like a small thing, but it can really get in my head.\n\nAnd then almost immediately I connect that to performance too. Like, I start thinking if I’m softer or heavier, then I’m going to be slower moving around at work — getting in and out around cars, bending, reaching, being on my feet — like I won’t move as easily. Same thing with basketball. I picture myself going up and down the court and feeling heavier or not as quick.\n\nSo I guess the fear is both. The appearance part hits first, but then I justify it to myself as, “No, this is about staying quick and able to do what I need to do.” It all kind of feeds into the same worry.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still want to lose weight or maintain your current weight if you were unable to see yourself in mirrors or reflections?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Maybe not in the exact same way, because mirrors definitely make me notice stuff, like my waist or how a shirt fits. But even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still have the scale, and I’d still feel my clothes.

Like if I stepped on the scale and it was up even a pound, I know myself — I’d start thinking, “Okay, what’s going on, am I getting soft?” And if my jeans felt tighter, especially around the waist, that would probably bother me even without seeing it. It’s not just the reflection. It’s that feeling of being bigger or heavier, and then my head goes right to, “I’m going to move slower.”

At work, I’m always moving around cars, getting into tight spots, bending, reaching, being on my feet all day. And with basketball, I think about running up and down the court and whether I feel quick or like I’m dragging. So even if there were no mirrors, I think I’d still want to keep my weight where it is, or maybe lose if I thought it was creeping up. The mirror is part of it, but it’s not the whole thing.

And at the garage, crawling around, bending, being under cars, moving around all day… I don’t feel as weighed down, you know? So it’s a little weird to be told it’s an issue when to me it’s felt more like I’m moving better. But they wanted me to follow up, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I get on the scale and it’s even up a pound, I notice it right away. Or if my jeans feel tighter around the waist, I start thinking, okay, I’m getting soft again, I’m losing that edge.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there scared in the corner, but it gets in my head. I start thinking about work, like crawling under cars, moving around the shop, getting up and down off the floor, and I picture myself being slower or more awkward. So yeah, if I wasn’t worried about how eating more might change how I look or feel in my body, I probably wouldn’t be so strict about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape, not just your weight or performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I don’t always separate it out like “shape” versus weight or performance. So yeah, I guess I do think about how food might change my body shape, not just the weight number.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever choose what to eat based on whether you think it will help you maintain a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t always sit there and say it that clearly to myself, like “I’m choosing this because it’ll keep me a certain shape,” but that’s kind of what it is.\n\nLike if I’m looking at what to eat, I’m thinking about whether it’s going to make me feel heavier or softer, especially around my middle. If the scale bumps up even a pound, I notice it right away, and then if my jeans feel tighter, that really sticks in my head. I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft,” and then I worry I’m not going to feel as quick or as comfortable moving around at work.\n\nI’m under cars, getting up and down, bending into tight spots, and I don’t like feeling bulky or slowed down. And same with playing basketball — if I feel heavier, I picture myself dragging up and down the court. So yeah, I guess I do pick the lighter or safer thing because I’m trying to keep that leaner shape, or at least avoid feeling like I’m getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can tell myself it’s about being practical or staying light because of work or basketball, but underneath that it’s mostly that I don’t want the weight to go up.\n\nIf I see the scale bump up even a pound, it really gets in my head. And then if my jeans feel tighter, especially around the waist, I start thinking, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” It’s not just some random number to me — I start picturing it affecting how I move, like I’ll be slower getting around cars at work, or less quick getting up and down off the floor, or dragging more when I’m running up and down the court.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say that fear is probably the main thing that makes me pull back on eating. I don’t always want to admit that, because it sounds kind of… I don’t know, maybe too much. But that’s the thought that usually pushes me to keep it lighter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating certain foods because you think they might change your appearance in a way you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do avoid some things for that reason. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “this food will change my appearance” in a really exact way, but it’s more like I picture what it’s going to do to me after I eat it.\n\nIf something feels too heavy, I start thinking it’s going to show up on me somehow, especially around my waist. And if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, then my mind goes right to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” I don’t like that feeling at all.\n\nThen it kind of turns into this whole thing where I’m not just thinking about how I look, but how I’ll move. Like, at work I’m getting in and out from under cars, bending, squeezing into spots, up and down off the floor all day, and if I feel bigger or softer I start thinking I’m going to be slower or awkward. Same with basketball — I picture myself dragging up and down the court instead of feeling quick.\n\nSo yeah, there are definitely foods I’ll skip because I’m worried they’ll make me look or feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Honestly, even the idea of not being able to control it makes me tense.\n\nIt’s like, if I see the scale bump up even one pound, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s normal” or let it go. I get stuck on it. Then I start checking how my jeans feel, especially around the waist, and if they feel tighter at all, my mind goes straight to, “Okay, I’m getting soft.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard to shake.\n\nIt’s not only about looking different, either. I start picturing myself moving worse — like being slower getting around cars at work, crawling under them, getting up and down off the floor, squeezing into tight spaces. Or on the court, running up and down and feeling like I’m dragging instead of quick. So if I couldn’t control my weight, yeah, I’d feel pretty anxious, because it would feel like I was losing control of how my body works and looks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, honestly. Even if I couldn’t see my body directly, I don’t think the fear would just disappear, because I’d still have other ways of noticing it.\n\nLike, if I saw the scale go up even a pound, that would still get in my head. Or if my jeans felt tighter around the waist, I’d start thinking the same thing I always do — that I’m getting soft. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s like I don’t need to actually see it in the mirror for my brain to start filling in the blanks.\n\nAnd then I’d start thinking about how it would affect me moving around, too. At work, I’m around cars all day, bending, getting in tight spaces, getting up and down, and I’d worry I’d be slower or less comfortable doing that. Same thing on the basketball court — I’d picture myself not being as quick, feeling heavier running up and down. So yeah, even without seeing my body, I think the fear would still be there because I’d feel like the weight was changing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is a difference. And I still play ball on the weekends, so it’s hard for me to see it the way they seem to see it.\n\nLike, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that. At work, I’m moving around cars all day, bending, getting into tight spots, getting up and down, and I start worrying I’ll be slower or less able to move the way I need to. And with basketball, I picture myself running up and down the court and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. It starts with how I think I look, but it turns into this fear that my body won’t perform right.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to stay at your current weight more about looking a certain way or being able to perform physically at your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it doesn’t stay as just one thing in my head. And on the court, same thing — I picture myself running up and down and feeling heavier, not as quick.\n\nSo staying where I am feels like… I don’t know, like I’m keeping myself sharp, I guess.
